Besov regularity of multivariate non-periodic functions in terms of half-period cosine coefficients and consequences for recovery and numerical integration
Martin Schäfer, Tino Ullrich

Abstract
In the setting of d d -variate periodic functions, often modelled as functions on the torus 𝕋 d ≅ [ 0 , 1 ] d \mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[0,1]^{d} , the classical tensorized Fourier system is the system of choice for many applications. Turning to non-periodic functions on the unit cube [ 0 , 1 ] d [0,1]^{d} the Fourier system is not as well-suited as exemplified by the Gibbs phenomenon at the boundary. Other systems have therefore been considered for such functions. One example is the half-period cosine system, which occurs naturally as the eigenfunctions of the Laplace operator under homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions. We introduce and analyze associated function spaces, S p , q r ​ B hpc ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right) , of dominating mixed Besov-type generalizing earlier concepts in this direction, in particular the half-period cosine spaces from [ 16 , 21 ] . As a main result, we show that there is a natural parameter range, where S p , q r ​ B hpc ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right) coincides with the classical Besov space of dominating mixed smoothness S p , q r ​ B ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d}) . This finding has direct implications for different functional analytic tasks in S p , q r ​ B ​ ( [ 0 , 1 ] d ) S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d}) . It allows to systematically transfer methods, originally taylored to the periodic domain, to the non-periodic setup. To illustrate this, we investigate half-period cosine approximation, sampling reconstruction, and tent-transformed cubature. Concerning cubature, for instance, we are able to reproduce the optimal convergence rate n − r ​ ( log ⁡ n ) ( d − 1 ) ​ ( 1 − 1 / q ) n^{-r}(\log n)^{(d-1)(1-1/q)} for tent-transformed digital nets in the range 1 ≤ p , q ≤ ∞ 1\leq p,q\leq\infty , 1 p < r < 2 \tfrac{1}{p}<r<2 , where n n is the number of samples. In our main proof we rely on Chui-Wang discretization of the dominating mixed Besov space S p , q r ​ B ​ ( ℝ d ) S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d}) . This paper therefore also includes an extension of [ 15 , Thm. 5.1] to the multivariate domain.



Keywords :
Besov space, Chui-Wang wavelets, Half-period cosine basis, Tent transform, Cubature, Recovery.

2010 Mathematics Subject Classification :
41A10, 41A25, 42C10, 42C40, 42B05, 42B35, 46E35, 65D32.

1
Introduction

Fourier series expansions of the form

\displaystyle f=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{f}(k)\exp_{k}=\hat{f}(0)+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\hat{f}_{c}(k)\cos_{k}+\hat{f}_{s}(k)\sin_{k}\big)

(1)

have been a well-established and prevalent tool in mathematics
since the beginnings of Fourier analysis in the 19th century, see e.g.
[
19
]
.
Their elementary building blocks, the exponentials
and trigonometric functions,
have proven to be well-suited for
the approximation of periodic functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, often modelled as functions
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
on the torus
\mathbb{T}
, if those are sufficiently smooth. Furthermore, the
smoothness of
f
is handily encoded in the decay of the Fourier coefficients,
a fact that is the foundation of the Fourier analytic characterization of a great variety of function spaces.
The tremendous success
of the expansions (
1
)
as a numerical tool, as pointed out in
[
33
]
,
can be traced back to
mainly two features:

(F1)

The fast decay of the Fourier coefficients for smooth
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
leading to a fast convergence of (
1
) to
f
.

(F2)

The stability of the expansion (
1
)
with respect to perturbations of the Fourier coefficients and, again for smooth
f
, the good numerical approximability of the latter.

As for (F1), the Fourier coefficients decay of order
\mathcal{O}(k^{-r-1})

if
f\in C^{r}(\mathbb{T})
and the
r
th derivative
f^{(r)}
is of bounded variation. Under the assumption of analyticity the decay is even exponential.
As for (F2), good stability properties of the expansion (
1
) are, for example, given with respect to uniform convergence. Here the approximation is stable with respect to perturbations of the coefficients in the
\ell_{1}
-norm. The latter can be numerically approximated via cubature with the discrete Fourier transform (DFT). If
f
is analytic the
deviation of the approximate values to the true coefficients decays exponentially in the number of samples.
The DFT, finally, can be efficiently computed by the fast Fourier transform (FFT).
To recall such Fourier analytic results we refer to the standard literature (e.g.
[
24
,
49
]
).

The short recapitulation above illustrates that
the numerical utility of the expansions (
1
) rests on a number of factors,
which unfortunately disappear
when turning to non-periodic functions
f:I\to\mathbb{C}

on a bounded interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
. Considering for example

I=[0,1]
,
the decay rate of
the Fourier coefficients breaks down to
\mathcal{O}(k^{-1})
if
f(0)\neq f(1)
, even for
highly smooth functions such as
f\in C^{\infty}([0,1])
. Likewise, the approximability of the coefficients via the DFT drops sharply to a rate of only
\mathcal{O}(n^{-2})
in the number of samples
n
.
The reason for this breakdown is the discrepancy of the function values at the endpoints of
I
, which correspond to jump discontinuities of the periodized function

\displaystyle f_{\rm per}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}\;,\quad f_{\rm per}(x):=f(x\bmod 1)\,.

(2)

Gibbs’ phenomenon, materializing at these jumps,
prohibits a fast convergence of (
1
). The slow decay of the Fourier coefficients further disrupts the correlation between the coefficients’ decay rate and the smoothness of
f
,
preventing straight-forward Fourier analytic characterization of smoothness.

Due to these deficiencies,
other approaches are necessary when dealing with non-periodic functions on compact domains. On the one hand, there are a variety of periodization strategies which aim to map functions to smooth periodic counterparts so that classical Fourier analysis can be applied efficiently. In this category fall domain transformation techniques, as analyzed e.g. in
[
43
,
44
,
45
,
46
]
, as well as extension methods enlarging the domain and extending the functions smoothly to the boundary, see e.g.
[
7
,
27
,
4
]
. A combination of both techniques is used in
[
50
]
. Another classical, very elegant approach is the polynomial subtraction method
[
36
,
51
]
, where a Hermite interpolant is subtracted from
f
to enhance the approximation (cf. Lanczos representation
[
41
]
).

On the other hand, a more direct solution is to look for suitable substitutes of the Fourier system on the given domain. Examples here are the Chebyshev polynomials on
[-1,1]
or systems of localized functions such as e.g. wavelet systems, see e.g.
[
35
,
14
]
. Often such systems, like for instance the Chebyshev polynomials, are the dual counterparts of corresponding periodization procedures. The same holds true for
the half-period cosine system, which is a simple modification of the Fourier basis on
[0,1]
, see the definition in Subsection
1.2
and Section
2
for the associated periodization, and the basis for our considerations in this paper. Whereas it may not be as powerful for approximation purposes as other more sophisticated function systems,
its conceptual proximity to the original Fourier system has many advantages. For instance, it inherits many features,
among them the validity of Fejér and de la Vallée-Poussin type theorems ensuring pointwise convergence of associated expansions in a broad spectrum of situations.

1.1
Modified Fourier systems

The half-period cosine system
and other similarly modified Fourier systems
have a long history in the theory of differential equations.
They naturally appear in the solution of Sturm-Liouville eigenfunction problems. The half-period cosine system, for instance, explicitly appeared as early as 1910 in Haar’s dissertation
[
23
]
, which is nowadays more famous for the introduction of the Haar wavelet, as a specific solution to the Sturm-Liouville problem with homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.
In the numerical approximation community these systems gained attention much later, notably in 2008 after Iserles and Nørsett showed good numerical properties of a modified Fourier basis in
L_{2}([-1,1])
.
In
[
33
]
the named authors considered the problem of approximating analytic functions
f
on the domain
[-1,1]
, where the Fourier system

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}([-1,1]):=\big\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big\}\cup\big\{\cos(\pi k\cdot)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\cup\>\,\big\{\sin(\pi k\cdot)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

(3)

is an orthonormal basis. They
noticed that the decay rate of the cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{c}(k)
and that of the sine coefficients
\hat{f}_{s}(k)
differ by an order of
1
. Indeed, a calculation of these coefficients via partial integration reveals that the coefficients
\hat{f}_{s}(k)
decay by an order of
1
slower than the coefficients
\hat{f}_{c}(k)
if
f(-1)\neq f(1)
:

\displaystyle|\hat{f}_{c}(k)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(\pi k)^{2}}\big[f^{\prime}(x)\big]_{x=-1}^{x=1}\>-\>\frac{1}{(\pi k)^{2}}\int\limits_{-1}^{1}f^{\prime\prime}(x)\cos(\pi kx)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\>\>\>\in\>\>\mathcal{O}(k^{-2})\,,

\displaystyle|\hat{f}_{s}(k)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{\pi k}\big[f(x)\big]_{x=-1}^{x=1}\>-\>\frac{1}{\pi k}\int\limits_{-1}^{1}f^{\prime}(x)\cos(\pi kx)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\>\>\>\in\>\>\mathcal{O}(k^{-1})\,.

To improve the overall rate,
Iserles and Nørsett therefore suggested to replace the
sine functions in (
3
) and came up with the modified system

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}([-1,1]):=\big\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big\}\cup\big\{\cos(\pi k\cdot)\,\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\cup\>\,\big\{\sin(\pi(k-\tfrac{1}{2})\cdot)\,\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,.

By their modification, the orthonormality of the system is not disturbed.
Moreover, the decay rate of the modified sine coefficients
\hat{f}_{s}^{\rm mod}(k)

now matches that of the cosine coefficients as the following calculation shows,

\displaystyle|\hat{f}_{s}^{\rm mod}(k)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(\pi(k-\tfrac{1}{2}))^{2}}\big[f^{\prime}(x)\big]_{x=-1}^{x=1}\>-\>\frac{1}{(\pi(k-\tfrac{1}{2}))^{2}}\int\limits_{-1}^{1}f^{\prime\prime}(x)\sin(\pi(k-\tfrac{1}{2})x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\>\>\>\in\>\>\mathcal{O}(k^{-2})\,.

Altogether, one thus gains
1
order in the decay rate of the coefficients resulting in an order
1
improvement in the approximation performance.
As Iserles and Nørsett themselves note,
the system
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
has appeared earlier in the literature, e.g. in 1935
in a publication by Krein
[
37
]
or in Young’s proof of the Kadec
\tfrac{1}{4}
-theorem
[
62
]
from 1980.
However, a systematic and comprehensive analysis was lacking,
specifically with respect to features (F1) and (F2).
This motivated
[
33
]
, which also served as a starting point for a series of follow-up papers
[
5
,
34
,
28
,
29
]
.

The main accomplishments in
[
33
]
are the analysis of
pointwise convergence of expansions in
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
, see also Olver
[
47
]
.
It is further shown that instead of approximating the coefficients by the DFT one can efficiently compute them via asymptotic expansions or quadrature techniques for highly oscillatory integrals, developed e.g. in
[
31
,
32
,
30
]
.
This addresses feature (F2), where one should add that the stability properties of expansions in
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
obviously coincide with those of ordinary Fourier expansions.
Concerning (F1), the coefficient decay is still rather unsatisfactory. While clearly an improvement over
\mathcal{F}([-1,1])
, it is limited to
\mathcal{O}(k^{-2})
even for analytic
f
if
f(-1)\neq f(1)
.
To obtain higher rates, higher order analogues of
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
can be used
based on
Birkhoff expansions. Such expansions were first considered in 1908 by Birkhoff
[
6
]
in the theory of differential equations, see also
[
55
,
42
]
.
For a suitable choice of differential operator and boundary conditions the resulting system of eigenfunctions allows for orthogonal expansions with a rapid decay of the expansion coefficients. A possible choice, as e.g. used in
[
34
,
2
]
, are the polyharmonic operators and homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions.

1.2
The half-period cosine system

The half-period cosine system considered in this paper is closely related to
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
. In the
1
-dimensional case, it consists of the functions
c_{k}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle c_{0}(\cdot):=1\quad\text{and}\quad c_{k}(\cdot):=\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi k\cdot)\quad\text{for }k\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(4)

It is a substitute for the orthonormal Fourier basis

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}([0,1]):=\big\{1\big\}\cup\big\{\sqrt{2}\cos(2\pi k\cdot)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\cup\>\,\big\{\sqrt{2}\sin(2\pi k\cdot)\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

on the interval
[0,1]
and subsequently denoted by
\mathcal{C}([0,1])
. It differs from the Fourier basis in that respect that in
\mathcal{C}([0,1])
the sine functions of
\mathcal{F}([0,1])
are replaced by the half-period cosine functions
\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi k\cdot)
,
k\in\{1,3,\ldots\}
.

The relation between the two systems can be described by the transformation
f\mapsto\sqrt{\tau_{1}^{\prime}(\cdot)}f(\tau_{1}(\cdot))
, which maps functions
f
on
[-1,1]
to functions on
[0,1]
, where
\tau_{1}
is the
linear assignment

\displaystyle\tau_{1}:[0,1]\to[-1,1]\;,\quad\tau_{1}(x):=2x-1\,.

(5)

Applied to
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
, this transformation leads to the system transformation

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\mapsto\,1\;,\quad\cos(\pi k\cdot)\,\mapsto\,(-1)^{k}\sqrt{2}\cos(2\pi k\cdot)\;,\quad\sin(\pi(k-\tfrac{1}{2})\cdot)\,\mapsto\,(-1)^{k+1}\sqrt{2}\cos(\pi(2k-1)\cdot)\,.

One can see that, up to the switching signs,
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
is transformed to
\mathcal{C}([0,1])
. The latter
can hence be considered the corresponding system on the domain
[0,1]
.
It features the same advantageous properties as
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
.
On the downside, its approximation performance is subject to the same limitations.

Since we work on a
d
-dimensional cube
[0,1]^{d}
, with
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we need a
d
-variate version of this system. It is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{d}:=\mathcal{C}([0,1]^{d}):=\big\{c_{\bar{k}}(\cdot)\big\}_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}}

(6)

and has as elements the tensor products
c_{\bar{k}}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

of the functions
c_{k}
from (
4
). Indexed by multi-indices
\bar{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}
, those are defined as

\displaystyle c_{\bar{k}}(x):=c_{k_{1}}(x_{1})\cdots c_{k_{d}}(x_{d})\quad\text{for }x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,.

More details
of the system
\mathcal{C}_{d}
are recalled and proved in Section
2
. Note that in
[
34
]

the modified Fourier system
\mathcal{M}([-1,1])
has also been extended to the multivariate cube
[-1,1]^{d}
. A short summary on this multivariate system
\mathcal{M}([-1,1]^{d})
is given by Adcock and Huybrechs in
[
3
]
. Further properties are proved by Adcock in
[
1
]
.

1.3
Periodization and the tent transform

An important tool in the analysis of the half-period cosine system is the so-called tent transform.
In dimension
d=1
this is the function

\displaystyle\phi_{1}:[0,1]\to[0,1]\;,\quad\phi_{1}(t):=1-|2t-1|\,,

whose graph looks like a tent. In some papers it is called baker’s transform (e.g. in
[
25
]
) due to its stretching and folding of the interval
[0,1]
reminiscent of the way a baker handles the bread dough. On the hypercube
[0,1]^{d}
the tent transform
\phi:[0,1]^{d}\to[0,1]^{d}
is the function that applies
\phi_{1}
to each coordinate.

Its relevance in the analysis of the system (
6
) comes from the fact that
it connects the half-period cosine functions with the
corresponding full-period functions. The precise relation is

\displaystyle c_{\bar{k}}\circ\phi=2^{|\bar{k}|_{0}/2}\cos(2\pi k_{1}\cdot)\cdots\cos(2\pi k_{d}\cdot)\;,\quad\bar{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\,,

(7)

where
|\bar{k}|_{0}
denotes the number of non-zero entries of
\bar{k}
.
The transformation
f\circ\phi
can be viewed as a periodization procedure
that takes
f
from the non-periodic domain
[0,1]^{d}
to the periodic domain
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[0,1]^{d}
.
Comparing it to the standard periodization, given by (
2
) in the univariate case,
more regularity is preserved than simple repetitions of the function would.
The transform
\phi
is a central element of the periodization principle concretized in Section
2
, which is a formal connection between half-period cosine analysis of non-periodic functions on the cube
[0,1]^{d}
and classical Fourier analysis of periodic functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Important applications of
\phi
have particularly been found in sampling theory, where it is used to transform node
sets
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset[0,1]^{d}
to so-called tent-transformed node sets
\{\phi(x_{i})\}_{i\in I}\subset[0,1]^{d}
.
In a non-periodic setting, using the latter often improves the performance of cubature rules or sampling reconstruction schemes. The corresponding procedures are called tent-transformed procedures. Taking the viewpoint that instead of the points the
functions are transformed, the better performance of the tent-transformed procedures can be explained by the higher regularity preserved under periodization by reflection compared to periodization by repetition.
Hickernell, in 2002, was the first who realized the advantages of the tent transform in the analysis of cubature rules.
In
[
25
, Thm. 3]
he used it
as a tool to prove
\mathcal{O}(n^{-2+\varepsilon})

convergence of tent-transformed randomly shifted lattice rules in certain Hilbert spaces, including
H^{2}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
. Hereby,
n
is the number of utilized samples. Goda, Suzuki, and Yoshiki later, in 2019, obtained the same rate
[
21
, Thm. 2, Cor. 1]

for tent-transformed lattice rules without shifting, building upon the investigations by Dick, Kuo, Nuyens, Pillichshammer, and Suryanarayana in
[
16
,
11
]
.
Similarly, for tent-transformed digital nets with random digital shifts,
\mathcal{O}(n^{-2+\varepsilon})
convergence was shown by Cristea, Dick, Leobacher, and Pillichshammer
[
12
, Thm. 5]
in 2007. Another area, where the tent transform is useful in applications, is function reconstruction and approximation from point samples.
This topic has been considered e.g. in
[
11
,
39
]
.

For technical reasons,
we will in the sequel in most cases parameterize the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
by
[-1,1]^{d}
instead of
[0,1]^{d}
. Instead of
\phi
, we then use the operator
\mathcal{P}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}f(x):=f(|x_{1}|,\ldots,|x_{d}|)\;,\quad x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in[-1,1]^{d}\,.

(8)

Like
\phi
, it shall be viewed as an operator that periodizes functions
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
by mapping them to the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
. It reflects them along the coordinate axes and
is related to the tent transform by the equality

\displaystyle f\circ(1-\phi)=(\mathcal{P}f)\circ\tau\,,

(9)

where
\tau:=\tau_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tau_{1}
is the
d
-variate tensorized version of
\tau_{1}
from (
5
).

1.4
Half-period cosine function spaces

Half-period cosine coefficients can be used to define function classes based on the coefficients’ properties,
similar as Fourier
coefficients.
For
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
the half-period cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})
are calculated by the formula

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k}):=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f(x)c_{\bar{k}}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(10)

A typical criterion is their decay and summability behaviour.
In
[
16
,
11
,
21
]
the
d
-variate half-period cosine space

\displaystyle\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}:=\Big\{f\in L_{2}([0,1]^{d})\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{\operatorname{C}}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}}:=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}|\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})|^{2}{\bf w}_{r,\bar{\gamma}}(\bar{k})<\infty\Big\}

is defined for parameters
r>1/2
and
\bar{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{d})>0

with weights of the form

{\bf w}_{r,\bar{\gamma}}(\bar{k}):={\rm w}_{r,\gamma_{1}}(k_{1})\cdots{\rm w}_{r,\gamma_{d}}(k_{d})\;,\quad{\rm w}_{r,\gamma_{j}}(k):=\begin{cases}1\quad&,\,k=0\,,\\
|k|^{2r}/\gamma_{j}&,\,k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}\,.\end{cases}

It is related to a corresponding class of periodic functions based on classical Fourier coefficients, namely

\displaystyle\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}:=\Big\{f\in L_{2}([0,1]^{d})\penalty\ :\penalty\ \|f\|^{2}_{\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}}:=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\hat{f}(\bar{k})|^{2}{\bf w}_{r,\bar{\gamma}}(\bar{k})<\infty\Big\}

with

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\bar{k}):=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f(x)\exp(-2\pi\mathrm{i}\bar{k}\cdot x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

The latter
is called weighted Korobov space in
[
16
,
11
,
21
]
.
Noting that
(1+|k|)/2\leq\max\{1,|k|\}\leq 1+|k|
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and hence, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,

2^{-2r}\max\{1,\gamma_{j}\}^{-1}(1+|k|)^{2r}\leq{\rm w}_{r,\gamma_{j}}(k)\leq\min\{1,\gamma_{j}\}^{-1}(1+|k|)^{2r}\,,

this space is equivalent to the periodic
Sobolev space
H^{r}_{\rm mix}(\mathbb{T}^{d}):=S^{r}_{2,2}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
(see Definition
3.8
) of dominating mixed smoothness on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. The dimension weights
\bar{\gamma}
do not change
\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
in terms of equivalence.

For integer
r\in\mathbb{N}
, it is analyzed in
[
16
,
21
]
how the spaces
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
and
\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
, as well as their sum
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}+\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
, embed into the non-periodic Sobolev space
H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d}):=S^{r}_{2,2}B([0,1]^{d})
(see Definition
3.6
). Due to
H^{r}_{\rm mix}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\subset H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
, it clearly holds
\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}\subset H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
for
r\in\mathbb{N}
, which is the first observation by Dick, Nuyens, and Pillichshammer in
[
16
]
. For the half-period cosine space
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
, these authors then show
the strict embedding
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}\subsetneq H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
for integer
r>1
and the equivalence
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}\asymp H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
for
r=1
.
Their results are summarized in
[
16
, Thm. 1]
. Goda, Suzuki, and Yoshiki refine this analysis in
[
21
]
and show in detail how
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
and the sum
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}+\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
are located in
H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
. In particular, they prove
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}+\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}\subsetneq H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
for integer
r>1
, which was left open in
[
16
]
. A summary of their results is given in
[
21
, Thm. 1]
.

The idea to use the coefficients (
10
) to define function classes is universal and not restricted to the class
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
.
A different kind of half-period cosine
space, but in the same spirit as
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
, has been considered recently in
[
39
]
.
It contains all functions on
[0,1]^{d}
with absolutely convergent half-period cosine series.
The main motivation for
such spaces are their natural properties, for instance,
with respect to tent-transformed lattice rules or half-period cosine approximation.
A main result of
[
16
]
is the proof of the existence of lattice rules for tent-transformed
QMC integration in
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
with a quasi-optimal rate of
\mathcal{O}(n^{-r+\varepsilon})
, where
\varepsilon>0
can be
arbitrarily small.

1.5
Our contribution

We pick up the ideas from
[
16
,
11
,
21
]

to define half-period cosine
spaces and introduce and systematically analyze a corresponding Besov-type scale, denoted by
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
.
This scale includes the class
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
recalled in the previous subsection.
It holds
\operatorname{C}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}\asymp S^{r}_{2,2}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
in the sense of equivalent norms. The periodic counterparts
of
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
are the spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})

of dominating mixed smoothness on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. They correspond to
\operatorname{E}_{d,r,\bar{\gamma}}
from the previous subsection in the Hilbert case
p=q=2
.

In the first main part of this article,
Section
3
, we give the definition
of
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
in Definition
3.12
. Hereby the complete range
0<p,q\leq\infty
is covered,
but we restrict the regularity to
r>\sigma_{p}
. This shall guarantee the embedding into proper function spaces and avoids technical difficulties in connection with the handling of distributions. For most practical applications, in sampling theory for instance, this restriction is not severe and even natural.
The main result of this section,
Theorem
3.13
, builds a bridge from
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
to periodic functions given by the bounded isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:\;S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)\cong S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}\quad\text{if }\sigma_{p}:=\max\{0,\tfrac{1}{p}-1\}<r\,.

The periodization
\mathcal{P}
is the operator from (
8
) and
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}
the subspace of
d
-fold even
functions in
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. The notion of
d
-fold even
function is introduced in Definition
2.3
.
Theorem
3.13
is a periodization principle, which
provides a useful translation mechanism from the non-periodic to the periodic domain.
A notable consequence is that tent-transformed procedures in
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
exhibit the same performance as the original procedures in
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}
. An upper bound for such procedures is thus always the original performance in
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, a
fact that was already observed in
[
11
]
.

In
Subsection
3.3
,
we apply the periodization principle to half-period cosine approximation, sampling reconstruction, and tent-transformed cubature.
Using tent-transformed digital nets with
n
integration nodes, we deduce the
optimal convergence rate
n^{-r}(\log n)^{(d-1)(1-1/q)}
for cubature in
S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})
, provided the parameters are in the range
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
,
\tfrac{1}{p}<r<2
. As a special case, this implies the rate
n^{-r}(\log n)^{(d-1)/2}
in the non-periodic Sobolev space
H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
if
\tfrac{1}{p}<r<2
. In
Theorem
3.14
, we further analyze weighted least-squares approximation in
S^{3/2}_{2,1}B([0,1]^{d})
from
n
point samples. Here we obtain the asymptotic approximation rate
n^{-3/2}(\log n)^{2(d-1)}
for reconstruction in the half-period cosine basis. Due to the embedding
H^{3/2+\varepsilon}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})\hookrightarrow S^{3/2}_{2,1}B([0,1]^{d})
for
\varepsilon>0
, this approximation rate in particular holds true for functions
f\in H^{r}_{\rm mix}([0,1]^{d})
with
r>\tfrac{3}{2}
.

For the deduction of the above results,
in addition to the periodization principle, we use embeddings of the spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})
into the half-period cosine scale
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
. These embedding relations are analyzed in

Section
4
, the second main part of the paper.
Let us give here a short summary of the relevant results.
According to
Theorem
4.1
,
we have for all
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
r>\sigma_{p}
the continuous embedding

\displaystyle\operatorname{Id}:\;S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)\hookrightarrow S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})\,,

where
\operatorname{Id}
is the identity operator.
In
Theorem
4.2
a partial converse is shown, namely

\displaystyle\operatorname{Id}:\;S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})\hookrightarrow S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)\quad\text{in the restricted range}\quad\sigma_{p}<r<\min\{1+\tfrac{1}{p},2\}\,.

Both theorems together establish a range of equivalence that substantially extends the earlier findings in
[
16
, Thm. 1]
, where the authors restrict to integer smoothness
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
A relevant endpoint result for
r=\min\{1+\tfrac{1}{p},2\}
is obtained in
Theorem
4.4
. It is the embedding

\displaystyle\operatorname{Id}:\;S^{r}_{p,\min\{p,1\}}B([0,1]^{d})\hookrightarrow S^{r}_{p,\infty}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)\quad\text{if $\tfrac{1}{3}<p<1$ or $1<p\leq\infty$}\,,

where the case
p=1
remains open due to the limitations of our proof technique.

The last part of the paper,
Section
5
, is a little
detached from the rest.
It stands on its own and is concerned with Chui-Wang wavelet analysis, which we need for our main proof in Section
4
.
The central achievement,
Theorem
5.1
, yields Chui-Wang characterizations for
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in a certain parameter range.
Such characterizations have
been given
in
[
15
, Thm. 5.1]
for the
1
-dimensional case, however with an incorrect statement in the boundary case
p=\infty,q<\infty
.
We give a more thorough analysis here and handle the boundary cases with special care.

1.6
Basic notation

The symbols
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
have their usual meaning. Further, we use
\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\mathbb{N}_{-1}:=\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{-1\}
,
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}:=[0,\infty)
, and
\mathbb{R}_{>0}:=(0,\infty)
. For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we put
[d]:=\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
Multi-indices
\bar{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

are denoted with a bar on top to distinguish them from scalar indices. For such multi-indices we define
b^{\bar{\alpha}}:=b^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots b^{\alpha_{d}}
, where
b>0
. The notation
(x)_{\pm}
stands for either
\max\{x,0\}
or
\min\{x,0\}
in case
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
it stands for the vector where
(\cdot)_{\pm}
is applied to each component. Often the bracket in
(x)_{\pm}
is omitted for readability and the
\pm
may be put at the top or bottom of the symbol for convenience.
With
|x|_{p}
, where
0<p\leq\infty
, we denote the standard
p
-(quasi-)norm of
x
.
The quantity
|x|_{0}
gives the number of non-zero entries,
|x|
denotes the vector
(|x_{1}|,\ldots,|x_{d}|)
of the absolute coordinate values.
By
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})>0

we mean that each coordinate is positive.
The Euclidean inner product of
x
with another vector
y
is denoted by
x\cdot y
.
For two sequences
\{a_{n}\}_{n}
,
\{b_{n}\}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}
we write

a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if
a_{n}\leq c\,b_{n}
for a fixed constant
c>0
and all
n
. We write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
b_{n}\lesssim a_{n}
.

Throughout the paper, we will extensively use the bracket notation

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{D}:=\int_{D}f(x)g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

(11)

for the integral with respect to the Lebesgue measure over a domain
D
, where
D
is either the
d
-torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
or
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Since
D=[0,1]^{d}
is our
standard domain, we further abbreviate
\langle f,g\rangle:=\langle f,g\rangle_{[0,1]^{d}}
.
The torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is usually represented in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}

by the cube
[-1,1]^{d}
, with antipodal points on the boundary identified. At some places, which are indicated, we also use the identification
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[0,1]^{d}
.
The meaning of
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
then depends on the utilized identification.

The Lebesgue spaces
L_{p}(D)
,
0<p\leq\infty
, are defined as usual. They contain
all Lebesgue measurable functions
f:D\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
satisfying

\|f\|_{L_{p}(D)}:=\Big(\,\int_{D}|f(x)|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)^{1/p}<\infty\,,

with the usual modification in case
p=\infty
.
The space
C(D)
denotes the collection of all
continuous and bounded functions on
D
equipped with the uniform norm. For

r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the space
C^{r}(D)
is comprised of the
r
-times differentiable functions on
D
, and

C_{c}^{r}(D)
denotes the subspace of compactly supported functions.

For
f\in L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we use the following version of the Fourier transform,

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\xi)\exp(-2\pi\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

It is extended as usual to tempered distributions
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e. elements of the topological dual
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of rapidly decreasing functions.
Its inverse is denoted by
f^{\vee}
.
For the dual pairing between tempered distributions
f
and Schwartz functions
g
we write
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}
.
More generally, we denote with
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathscr{F}\times\tilde{\mathscr{F}}}
the duality product on pairs
\mathscr{F}\times\tilde{\mathscr{F}}
of conjugate spaces, see Definition
A.8
.

2
The half-period cosine system

Let us, in this preliminary section, inspect more closely the half-period cosine system
\mathcal{C}_{d}=\{c_{\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}}

defined in (
6
).
Hereby
a periodization principle plays a key role that connects non-periodic functions on the
d
-dimensional hypercube
[0,1]^{d}

with periodic functions on
[-1,1]^{d}
, however not in the standard way. It is realized by
the non-standard periodization operator
\mathcal{P}
from (
8
) based on reflections rather than repetitions of the original function. More regularity is preserved this way, for instance the continuity of the input function.

With the help of the auxiliary map

\displaystyle\rho_{1}:\>\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\;,\quad x\mapsto\min\big\{x\bmod 2,(-x)\bmod 2\big\}

(12)

and its multivariate extension
\rho:=\rho_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\rho_{1}
,
this periodization operator
\mathcal{P}
can be defined more conveniently than in (
8
) as follows.

Definition 2.1

(Periodization)
.

Let
f:D\to\mathbb{C}
be a function with
[0,1]^{d}\subset D
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}
the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:\;f\mapsto f\circ\rho|_{[-1,1]^{d}}\,.

Recall that we here identify
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
and therefore parameterize the
1
-dimensional torus
\mathbb{T}
by the extended unit interval
[-1,1]
, with endpoints identified.

The following restriction operator also plays an important role.

Definition 2.2

(Restriction)
.

Let
f:D\to\mathbb{C}
be a function with
[0,1]^{d}\subset D
.
We denote by
\mathcal{R}
the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:\;f\mapsto f|_{[0,1]^{d}}\,.

It is easy to check that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{R}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{P}\,.

These relations imply that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{R}
are pseudo-inverse to each other, fulfilling

\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}
. Furthermore, for periodizations of functions
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
via
\mathcal{P}
, i.e. applications of
\mathcal{P}
to functions
f
with domain
D=[0,1]^{d}
, the restriction operator
\mathcal{R}
from Definition
2.2
is a left-inverse.

2.1
Periodization principle

We are now ready to formulate the basic aspects of the periodization principle.
One observation is that the periodizations
\mathcal{P}(f)
lead to
d
-fold even functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with respect to the parametrization
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
.
Let us put this notion in a formal definition.

Definition 2.3
.

A function
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
is called
d
-fold even
if it satisfies

\displaystyle f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=f(\pm x_{1},\ldots,\pm x_{d})\quad\text{for all}\quad(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in[-1,1]^{d}\,.

It is obvious that

\mathcal{P}:\;L_{1}([0,1]^{d})\to L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

is an isometric operator with
\|\mathcal{P}(f)\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=2^{d}\|f\|_{L_{1}([0,1]^{d})}
.
Together with the observation that the image consists precisely of the
d
-fold even functions in
L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
we obtain the next lemma.

Lemma 2.4
.

The operator
\mathcal{P}
from Definition
2.1
is an isometric isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:\;L_{1}([0,1]^{d})\cong L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}\,.

Hereby
L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}
means the subspace of
L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

comprised of the
d
-fold even functions.

Let us next have a look at the restriction operator
\mathcal{R}:f\mapsto f|_{[0,1]^{d}}
from Definition
2.2
when applied to periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
[0,1]^{d}\subset[-1,1]^{d}
,
the interpretation
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
indeed allows to apply this operator to functions
f\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
One obtains
\mathcal{R}f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
with
\|\mathcal{R}f\|_{L_{1}([0,1]^{d})}\leq\|f\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
and hence
the following lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

The operator
\mathcal{R}
from Definition
2.2
is bounded as operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:\;L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to L_{1}([0,1]^{d})\,.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is a left-inverse to
\mathcal{P}
on
L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
, we see,
counting in Lemma
2.4
, that
\mathcal{P}
and

\mathcal{R}
establish a
1
-
1
-correspondence between

L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
and
L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}
. Utilizing these operators, one can move back and forth between these spaces and it is this translation mechanism which is the foundation of the periodization principle.

We next establish a relation of
\mathcal{C}_{d}=\{c_{\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}}
to the standard Fourier basis in
L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
consisting of the exponential functions
\exp_{\bar{k}}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
,
\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. With respect
to the parametrization
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
these
exponentials take the form

\displaystyle\exp_{\bar{k}}(x):=2^{-d/2}\exp(\mathrm{i}\pi\bar{k}\cdot x)\;,\quad x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\,.

The normalization constant
2^{-d/2}
ensures that the exponential system on
[-1,1]^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}([-1,1]^{d}):=\big\{\exp_{\bar{k}}\big\}_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\,,

(13)

is an orthonormal basis of
L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

In addition, we define
\cos_{\bar{k}}:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

for
\bar{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
via

\displaystyle\cos_{\bar{k}}(x)

\displaystyle:=2^{-d/2}\cos(\pi k_{1}x_{1})\cdots\cos(\pi k_{d}x_{d})\;,\quad x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\,.

As the next lemma states, the functions
c_{\bar{k}}
and
\cos_{\bar{k}}
are related by the periodization operator
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

For all
\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
it holds

\mathcal{P}(c_{|\bar{k}|})=2^{(|\bar{k}|_{0}+d)/2}\cos_{\bar{k}}\,,

where
|\bar{k}|_{0}
counts the number of non-zero entries of
\bar{k}

and
|\bar{k}|=(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{d}|)\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
.

Proof.

By definition,
c_{\bar{k}}(x)=2^{|\bar{k}|_{0}/2}\cos(\pi k_{1}x_{1})\cdots\cos(\pi k_{d}x_{d})
for
\bar{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
and
x\in[0,1]^{d}
. Inserting the definition of
\mathcal{P}
, we obtain for
x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(c_{\bar{k}})(x)=2^{|\bar{k}|_{0}/2}\cos(\pi k_{1}|x_{1}|)\cdots\cos(\pi k_{d}|x_{d}|)=2^{|\bar{k}|_{0}/2}\cos(\pi k_{1}x_{1})\cdots\cos(\pi k_{d}x_{d})\,.

Recalling the definition of
\cos_{\bar{k}}
and the symmetry
\cos_{\bar{k}}=\cos_{|\bar{k}|}
, we are finished.
∎

Let us remark that the formula in Lemma
2.6
is analogous to the connection, described in (
7
), of
c_{\bar{k}}
to the tensorized cosine functions
\cos(2\pi k_{1}\cdot)\cdots\cos(2\pi k_{d}\cdot)
on
[0,1]^{d}
via the tent transform, namely

c_{|\bar{k}|}\circ\phi=2^{|\bar{k}|_{0}/2}\cos(2\pi k_{1}\cdot)\cdots\cos(2\pi k_{d}\cdot)\,.

Finally, drawing a connection between
\cos_{\bar{k}}
and
\exp_{\bar{k}}
, note that by Euler’s formula we have the expansion

\displaystyle\exp_{\bar{k}}(x)=2^{-d/2}\prod_{j=1}^{d}\exp(\mathrm{i}\pi k_{j}x_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bar{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{d}}\mathrm{i}^{|\bar{\varepsilon}|_{1}}g_{\bar{k}}^{(\bar{\varepsilon})}(x)

\displaystyle=\cos_{\bar{k}}(x)+\sum_{\bar{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\,,|\bar{\varepsilon}|_{1}>0}\mathrm{i}^{|\bar{\varepsilon}|_{1}}g_{\bar{k}}^{(\bar{\varepsilon})}(x)\,,

(14)

where for each
\bar{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{d}

\displaystyle g_{\bar{k}}^{(\bar{\varepsilon})}(x):=g_{k_{1}}^{(\varepsilon_{1})}(x_{1})\cdots g_{k_{d}}^{(\varepsilon_{d})}(x_{d})\quad\text{with}\quad g_{k_{j}}^{(\varepsilon_{j})}(x_{j}):=\begin{cases}2^{-1/2}\cos(\pi k_{j}x_{j})&,\,\varepsilon_{j}=0\,,\\
2^{-1/2}\sin(\pi k_{j}x_{j})&,\,\varepsilon_{j}=1\,.\end{cases}

Hence, the function
\cos_{\bar{k}}
can be considered as the
d
-fold even part of
\exp_{\bar{k}}
.

2.2
Half-period cosine coefficients

The half-period cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})
of a function
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
have been defined in (
10
).
The computation of the Fourier coefficients of an integrable
d
-variate periodic function on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
is performed by the formula

\hat{f}(\bar{k})=2^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\bar{k}\cdot x}\,\mathrm{d}x\,,\quad\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,.

Using the bracket notation introduced in (
11
), we can write both coefficients short-hand as

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})=\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle\quad,\quad\hat{f}(\bar{k})=\langle f,\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\,.

We know from Lemma
2.4
that
\mathcal{P}(f)\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}
and
\mathcal{P}(g)\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}
for any
f,g\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
. Another
basic but important observation is that for any
f,g\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle=2^{-d}\langle\mathcal{P}(f),\mathcal{P}(g)\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\,.

(15)

From this fact and Lemma
2.6
we get the following result that relates
the Fourier coefficients of
\mathcal{P}(f)
to the half-period cosine coefficients of
f
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Recall that
|\bar{k}|_{0}
counts the non-zero entries of
\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and that
|\bar{k}|=(|k_{1}|,\ldots,|k_{d}|)\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}
. It holds

\displaystyle\langle f,c_{|\bar{k}|}\rangle=2^{(|\bar{k}|_{0}-d)/2}\langle\mathcal{P}(f),\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\,.

Proof.

Put
\tilde{f}:=\mathcal{P}(f)
. From Lemma
2.6
and (
15
) we get

\displaystyle\langle f,c_{|\bar{k}|}\rangle=2^{(|\bar{k}|_{0}-d)/2}\langle\tilde{f},\cos_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\,.

Further, we have according to the expansion (
14
)

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{f},\exp_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}=\langle\tilde{f},\cos_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}+\sum_{\bar{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{d},\,|\bar{\varepsilon}|_{1}>0}\langle\tilde{f},\mathrm{i}^{|\bar{\varepsilon}|_{1}}g_{\bar{k}}^{(\bar{\varepsilon})}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}=\langle\tilde{f},\cos_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\,.

Indeed, let
\bar{\varepsilon}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
with
|\bar{\varepsilon}|_{1}>0
, where without loss of generality we assume
\varepsilon_{1}=1
. Then

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{f},g_{\bar{k}}^{(\bar{\varepsilon})}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{x_{d}=-1}^{1}\cdots\int\limits_{x_{2}=-1}^{1}\bigg(\int\limits_{x_{1}=-1}^{1}\tilde{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})g_{k_{1}}^{(\varepsilon_{1})}(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\bigg)g_{k_{2}}^{(\varepsilon_{2})}(x_{2})\cdots g_{k_{d}}^{(\varepsilon_{d})}(x_{d})\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}x_{d}

and, since
\varepsilon_{1}=1
and
\tilde{f}
is even in each component,

\int\limits_{x_{1}=-1}^{1}\tilde{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})g_{k_{1}}^{(\varepsilon_{1})}(x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}=\int\limits_{x_{1}=-1}^{1}\tilde{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})2^{-1/2}\sin(\pi k_{1}x_{1})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}=0\,.

The final observation, which finishes the proof, is
\langle\tilde{f},\exp_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}=\langle\tilde{f},\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
due to
\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}=\exp_{-\bar{k}}
.
∎

2.3
Half-period cosine expansions

Since
\mathcal{C}_{d}=\{c_{\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}}
is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}([0,1]^{d})
each
f\in L_{2}([0,1]^{d})

has an orthonormal expansion

\displaystyle f=\sum_{\bar{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}}\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle c_{\bar{k}}

(16)

with
L_{2}
convergence and the coefficients satisfy
\{\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle\}_{\bar{k}}\in\ell_{2}(\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0})
.
We are interested in an analogous result for
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
.
The first observation is that, since
c_{\bar{k}}\in L_{\infty}([0,1]^{d})
, the half-period cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})=\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle
still make sense for every
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
. In this more general case, however, one merely has
\{\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})\}_{\bar{k}}\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0})
and the question of convergence in (
16
) arises.

We will show in Lemma
2.9
below that, when the functions
f
and
c_{\bar{k}}
are considered as functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
trivially extended by
0
’s outside of
[0,1]^{d}
, one has weak* convergence in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the space of tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
To prove Lemma
2.9
, we first analyze the decay of the
half-period cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})
of functions
f=\eta\cdot\chi_{[0,1]^{d}}
, where
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Schwartz function.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\left\langle x\right\rangle:=(1+|x|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
be the ‘analyst’s bracket’. For every
\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
we have the estimate

\left\lvert\left\langle\eta,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle k_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\\
\left\lvert\bar{\alpha}\right\rvert_{\infty}\leq 2\end{subarray}}\left\lVert\partial^{\bar{\alpha}}\eta\right\rVert_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

(17)

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the dimension
d
. Our starting point is the case
d=1
. Take
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. Then

\left\lvert\left\langle\eta,c_{0}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\int\limits_{0}^{1}\left\lvert\eta(x)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\,.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
we get, integrating by parts twice,

\left\langle\eta,c_{k}\right\rangle=\int\limits_{0}^{1}\eta(x)c_{k}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\pi^{2}k^{2}}\left((-1)^{k}\partial\eta(1)-\partial\eta(0)\right)-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}k^{2}}\int\limits_{0}^{1}c_{k}(x)\partial^{2}\eta(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Consequently, we have (
17
) in the univariate case.
Let us next assume that (
17
) is true up to dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
. If
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
we
subsequently write
x=(\widehat{x},t)
with
\widehat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Similarly, we write
\bar{k}=(\widehat{k},l)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
for
\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d+1}
.
Considering
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})
, we have

\left\langle\eta,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle=\left\langle c_{\widehat{k}}(\widehat{x})c_{l}(t),\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rangle=\left\langle c_{l}(t),\left\langle c_{\widehat{k}}(\widehat{x}),\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rangle_{\widehat{x}}\right\rangle_{t}\,,

where
c_{\widehat{k}}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{d}c_{k_{i}}
. The map
t\mapsto\left\langle c_{\widehat{k}}(\widehat{x}),\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rangle_{\widehat{x}}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
and so our previous calculations yield

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle c_{\bar{k}},\eta\right\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim\frac{1}{\left\langle l\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\lvert\left\langle c_{\widehat{k}}(\widehat{x}),\partial_{t}^{\beta}\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rangle_{\widehat{x}}\right\rvert\,,

where, by induction hypothesis,

\left\lvert\left\langle c_{\widehat{k}}(\widehat{x}),\partial_{t}^{\beta}\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rangle_{\widehat{x}}\right\rvert\lesssim\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle k_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\\
\left\lvert\widehat{\alpha}\right\rvert_{\infty}\leq 2\end{subarray}}\sup_{\widehat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\partial_{t}^{\beta}\partial_{\widehat{x}}^{\widehat{\alpha}}\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rvert\,.

Hence, we obtain altogether

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle c_{\bar{k}},\eta\right\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim\frac{1}{\left\langle l\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{\beta=0}^{2}\,\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle k_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\\
\left\lvert\widehat{\alpha}\right\rvert_{\infty}\leq 2\end{subarray}}\sup_{\widehat{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert\partial_{t}^{\beta}\partial_{\widehat{x}}^{\widehat{\alpha}}\eta(\widehat{x},t)\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\left\langle l\right\rangle^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle k_{i}\right\rangle^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d+1}\\
\left\lvert\bar{\alpha}\right\rvert_{\infty}\leq 2\end{subarray}}\left\lVert\partial^{\bar{\alpha}}\eta\right\rVert_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1})}\,.

Our proof is complete.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Lemma
2.9
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
and identify
f
with its trivial extension to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then we have

f=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}\quad\text{weak*ly in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

Proof.

Take
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\mathcal{P}(\eta)\in L_{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for the periodization operator
\mathcal{P}
from Definition
2.1
and, by the multivariate version of the celebrated Carleson-Hunt theorem, see e.g.
[
22
, Thm 4.3.16]
, the function
\mathcal{P}(\eta)
coincides with its Fourier series pointwise almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Due to symmetry, this Fourier series is equal to the half-period cosine series of
\eta
on
[0,1]^{d}
. In other words, on the unit cube the latter converges to
\eta
pointwise almost everywhere. We thus have

\eta(x)=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\left\langle\eta,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}(x)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{|\bar{k}|_{\infty}\leq N}\left\langle\eta,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}(x)\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in[0,1]^{d}\,.

(18)

Consider now an arbitrary
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
and identify it with its trivial extension to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\Big\langle f-\sum_{|\bar{k}|_{\infty}\leq N}\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}},\eta\Big\rangle\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\Big(f(x)-\sum_{|\bar{k}|_{\infty}\leq N}\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}(x)\Big)\,\eta(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left\lvert f(x)\right\rvert\,\bigg|\eta(x)-\sum_{|\bar{k}|_{\infty}\leq N}\left\langle\eta,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}(x)\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}x\>\longrightarrow 0\quad(N\longrightarrow\infty)\,.

The convergence in the last step follows from Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, since (
18
) holds true and, due to Lemma
2.8
, the
function

f(\cdot)\Big(\|\eta\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\sum\limits_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}|\langle\eta,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle|\|c_{\bar{k}}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)

is an integrable majorant.
∎

2.4
Further aspects of the periodization principle

In this final part of Section
2
we want to present some additional aspects of the periodization principle from an applications’ point of view.
In contrast to the rest of the paper, the identification
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[0,1]^{d}
will be used here and the tent transform
\phi
instead of
\mathcal{P}
for the periodization. This
has the advantage that then both
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
[0,1]^{d}
have unit measure. It is further more common in the half-period cosine literature (see e.g.
[
16
,
11
,
21
]
).
The tent transform
\phi
was explained in Subsection
1.3
.
We set
f^{\phi}:=f\circ\phi
for functions
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
.

As our first application, let us consider the problem of
L_{p}
approximation of functions
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
.
Hereby we assume that we have a selection rule
\sigma:\mathbb{N}\to\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d})
for the half-period cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})
of
f
, where
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d})
denotes the
power set of
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
. The
N
th half-period cosine approximant of
f
with respect to
\sigma
is given by

\displaystyle A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}f(x):=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\sigma(N)}\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})c_{\bar{k}}(x)\,.

For periodic functions
g\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
we further define the
N
th Fourier approximant

\displaystyle A^{(\sigma)}_{N}g(x):=\sum_{|\bar{k}|\in\sigma(N)}\langle g,\exp(-2\pi\mathrm{i}\bar{k}\cdot\,)\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\exp(2\pi\mathrm{i}\bar{k}\cdot x)

subject to the selection rule
\sigma
.
The following result then holds true.

Lemma 2.10
.

For
0<p\leq\infty

\displaystyle\|f-A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}f\|_{L_{p}([0,1]^{d})}=\|f^{\phi}-A^{(\sigma)}_{N}f^{\phi}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\,.

Proof.

Due to relation (
9
) between
\phi
and
\mathcal{P}
, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\|f-A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}f\|_{L_{p}([0,1]^{d})}=2^{-d/p}\|\mathcal{P}(f)-\widetilde{A}^{(\sigma)}_{N}[\mathcal{P}(f)]\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\,,

(19)

where here in the proof we let
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
as usual and set

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}^{(\sigma)}_{N}[\mathcal{P}(f)]:=\sum_{|\bar{k}|\in\sigma(N)}\langle\mathcal{P}(f),\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\exp_{\bar{k}}\,.

From Lemma
2.6
and Lemma
2.7
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}f)=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\sigma(N)}\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle\mathcal{P}(c_{\bar{k}})=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\sigma(N)}2^{|\bar{k}|_{0}}\langle\mathcal{P}(f),\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\cos_{\bar{k}}=\widetilde{A}^{(\sigma)}_{N}[\mathcal{P}(f)]\,.

Further

\displaystyle\|f-A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}f\|^{p}_{L_{p}([0,1]^{d})}=2^{-d}\|\mathcal{P}(f)-\mathcal{P}(A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}f)\|^{p}_{L_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=2^{-d}\|\mathcal{P}(f)-\widetilde{A}^{(\sigma)}_{N}[\mathcal{P}(f)]\|^{p}_{L_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\,,

which verifies (
19
).
∎

Lemma
2.10
provides the following insight:
For
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
an approximation scheme
\{A^{(\sigma)}_{{\rm hpc},N}\}_{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle{N\in\mathbb{N}}$}}
based on half-period cosine functions works precisely as well as the corresponding Fourier approximation scheme
\{A^{(\sigma)}_{N}\}_{\raisebox{-1.80835pt}{$\scriptstyle{N\in\mathbb{N}}$}}
for
f^{\phi}
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

The cubature of functions
f\in C([0,1]^{d})
is the second application we are interested in. Here one tries to approximate the integral

\displaystyle I(f):=\int_{[0,1]^{d}}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

from a finite number of function samples.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let us fix a set of sampling points

X_{n}=\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset[0,1]^{d}
and an associated weight set

\Lambda_{n}=\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}
.
The pair
(X_{n},\Lambda_{n})
determines a
cubature rule
\Lambda_{n}(X_{n},f)
for functions
f\in C([0,1]^{d})

by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(X_{n},f):=\sum_{x_{j}\in X_{n}}\lambda_{j}f(x_{j})\,.

The tent-transformed node set
X^{\phi}_{n}=\{x^{\phi}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}

is given by
x^{\phi}_{j}:=\phi(x_{j})
and
we call
\Lambda_{n}(X^{\phi}_{n},f)

the corresponding tent-transformed cubature rule for
f
.
We have the following result.

Lemma 2.11
.

It holds
|\Lambda_{n}(X_{n}^{\phi},f)-I(f)|=|\Lambda_{n}(X_{n},f^{\phi})-I(f^{\phi})|
.

Proof.

The statement follows from
I(f)=I(f^{\phi})
and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n}(X_{n}^{\phi},f)=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}f(x^{\phi}_{j})=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}f^{\phi}(x_{j})=\Lambda_{n}(X_{n},f^{\phi})\,.\qed

From Lemma
2.11
we learn that a tent-transformed cubature rule performs as well for a non-periodic function
f\in C([0,1]^{d})

as the original rule for the periodized function
f^{\phi}\in C(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Concrete applications of Lemmas
2.10
and
2.11
are provided in Subsection
3.3
.

3
Besov-type function spaces

The Fourier analytic definition of the classical univariate Besov spaces
B^{r}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})
is based on dyadic partitions of the Fourier domain. A central notion in this respect are
dyadic decompositions of unity
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
, i.e. families of non-negative functions
\varphi_{j}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
subject to the following three conditions:

(i)

There is
c>0
such that
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi_{0})\subset\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\left\lvert cx\right\rvert\leq 2\}
and
\operatorname{supp}(\varphi_{j})\subset\{x\in\mathbb{R}:2^{j-1}\leq\left\lvert cx\right\rvert\leq 2^{j+1}\}
for
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

(ii)

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exists a constant
c_{m}>0
such that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}2^{mj}\left\lvert\partial^{m}\varphi_{j}(x)\right\rvert\leq c_{m}
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\varphi_{j}(x)=1
at every point
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Dyadic decompositions like this give rise to corresponding decompositions of functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
, which are at the center of Littlewood-Paley theory and form the Fourier analytic basis for the construction of a wide range of function spaces, prominently among them the Besov and the Triebel-Lizorkin scale (see e.g.
[
58
,
52
]
).

Remark 3.1
.

The standard example of a dyadic decomposition of unity is as follows. One lets
\varphi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be non-negative with
\varphi_{0}(x)=1
on
[-1,1]
and
\varphi_{0}(x)=0
if
\left\lvert x\right\rvert>2
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
one further sets

\varphi_{j}(x):=\varphi_{0}(2^{-j}x)-\varphi_{0}(2^{-j+1}x)\,.

There are two main directions for the generalization of the univariate spaces
B^{r}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R})
to the multivariate domain: the isotropic Besov spaces
B^{r}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the Besov spaces of dominating mixed smoothness
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Both directions differ in the utilized concept of multivariate dyadic decompositions of unity on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
For the isotropic spaces
B^{r}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the Fourier domain is partitioned into rotation-symmetric dyadic annuli (see e.g.
[
58
,
52
]
).
The spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, on the other side,
follow the idea of tensorization (see e.g.
[
53
, Ch. 2]
or
[
61
, Ch. 1]
).

3.1
The classical Besov spaces of dominating mixed smoothness

In this paper we will
deal with the spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whose definition rests on multivariate partitions of unity obtained by tensorization.
Those are often referred to as hyperbolic in the literature. One starts with
d\in\mathbb{N}
, possibly different, dyadic partitions
\{\varphi^{(i)}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
on
\mathbb{R}
, indexed by
i\in[d]
, and then defines for
\bar{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}

\varphi_{\bar{j}}(x):=\varphi^{(1)}_{j_{1}}(x_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi^{(d)}_{j_{d}}(x_{d})\;,\quad x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in R^{d}\,.

The class of families
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
of such construction will subsequently be denoted
by
\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Associated to each family
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a family
\{\Phi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
of kernels
\Phi_{\bar{j}}
connected to
\varphi_{\bar{j}}
by the relation

\displaystyle\varphi_{\bar{j}}(x)=\widehat{\Phi}_{\bar{j}}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Phi_{\bar{j}}(\xi)\exp(-2\pi\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi\,.

(20)

Since
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
is contained in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

the kernels
\{\Phi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
are also elements of the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

3.1.1
The spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{d}

As mentioned in the beginning of this section,
dyadic decompositions of unity give rise to corresponding decompositions of functions.
More generally,
let us now consider tempered distributions
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, i.e. elements of the dual space of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and decompositions
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
The components of the decomposition of
f
are defined as
f_{\bar{j}}:=(\varphi_{\bar{j}}\widehat{f}\,)^{\vee}
and are called Littlewood-Paley blocks.
They can be written as convolutions

\displaystyle f_{\bar{j}}(x)=\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f(x):=\left\langle f,\Phi_{\bar{j}}(x-\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}

(21)

with the associated kernels
\Phi_{\bar{j}}
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}
denotes the duality product on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The corresponding decomposition of
f
,

\displaystyle f=\sum_{\bar{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}}f_{\bar{j}}=\sum_{\bar{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}}\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f\,,

(22)

which holds true with at least weak* convergence in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
is called Littlewood-Paley decomposition of
f
.
With this notion we are ready to give the definition of

S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 3.2

(cf.
[
53
, Def. 2]
,
[
60
, Def. 3.2.2]
)
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\{\Phi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
a family of kernels associated to
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The space
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as the collection of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for which

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\begin{cases}\Big(\sum\limits_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}2^{rq|\bar{j}|_{1}}\left\lVert\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f\right\rVert_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\;,\quad&q<\infty\;,\\[17.22217pt]
\displaystyle\sup_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}2^{r|\bar{j}|_{1}}\left\lVert\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f\right\rVert_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\;,\quad&q=\infty\;,\end{cases}

is finite. The expression
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
constitutes a (quasi-)norm in
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
do not depend on the choice of family
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the definition. Different families
lead to the same class of functions and equivalent (quasi-)norms. In case
\min\{p,q\}\geq 1
the
defined spaces are Banach spaces,
whereas they are quasi-Banach in case
u:=\min\{p,q\}<1
. More precisely, they are then

u
-Banach spaces since the quasi-norm satisfies the
u
-triangle inequality

\left\lVert f+g\right\rVert^{u}_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{u}_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\left\lVert g\right\rVert^{u}_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,.

For these facts and many other properties of these spaces we refer to the literature (e.g.
[
53
,
61
]
).

Let us also recall the concept of Besov-type sequence spaces. Spaces of this kind,
s^{r}_{p,q}b(\Gamma^{d})
with index set
\Gamma^{d}:=\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, naturally associated to
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
have been considered for
d=1
in
[
15
, Def. A.4]
.

Definition 3.3

(cf.
[
15
, Def. A.4]
and Remark
3.4
)
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
.
The space

s^{r}_{p,q}b(\Gamma^{d})
is
the collection of all sequences
\{\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\}_{(\bar{j},\bar{k})\in\Gamma^{d}}
in
\mathbb{C}
with index set
\Gamma^{d}:=\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}^{d}

such that

\|\{\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\|_{s^{r}_{p,q}b(\Gamma^{d})}:=\begin{cases}\Big(\sum\limits_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}}2^{|\bar{j}|_{1}(r-\frac{1}{p})q}\Big(\sum\limits_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\;,\quad&q<\infty\;,\\[17.22217pt]
\displaystyle\sup_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}}2^{\left\lvert\bar{j}\right\rvert_{1}(r-\frac{1}{p})}\Big(\sum\limits_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\;,\quad&q=\infty\;,\end{cases}

is finite. The expression
\|\{\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\|_{s^{r}_{p,q}b(\Gamma^{d})}
constitutes a (quasi-)norm.

Remark 3.4
.

If one defines
\chi_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}:=\chi_{\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{$\scriptstyle{Q_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}}$}}
as the characteristic function of the rectangle

\displaystyle Q_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}:=Q_{j_{1},k_{1}}\times\cdots\times Q_{j_{d},k_{d}}\quad\text{with}\quad Q_{j_{i},k_{i}}:=[2^{j_{i}^{+}}k_{i},2^{j_{i}^{+}}(k_{i}+1)]\quad\text{for }i\in[d]\,,

(23)

it holds, with the usual modification if
q=\infty
,

\displaystyle\|\{\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\|_{s^{r}_{p,q}b(\Gamma^{d})}\asymp\Big(\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{-1}}2^{|\bar{j}|_{1}rq}\Big\|\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|\chi_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}(\cdot)\Big\|^{q}_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\Big)^{1/q}\,.

A Hölder-type inequality is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r\in\mathbb{R}
, and
p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime}
the conjugate parameters as in Definition
A.7
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{(\bar{j},\bar{k})\in\Gamma^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\mu_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|\leq\|\{\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\|_{s^{r}_{p,q}b(\Gamma^{d})}\cdot\|\{\mu_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\|_{s^{r^{\prime}+1}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}b(\Gamma^{d})}\,.

Proof.

The proof is a straight-forward calculation. We give it for
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}<\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{(\bar{j},\bar{k})\in\Gamma^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}\mu_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}}\Big(\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\mu_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|^{p^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}}2^{|\bar{j}|_{1}(r-\frac{1}{p})q}\Big(\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\lambda_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{-1}^{d}}2^{|\bar{j}|_{1}(\frac{1}{p}-r)q^{\prime}}\Big(\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|\mu_{\bar{j},\bar{k}}|^{p^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{q^{\prime}}{p^{\prime}}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}}\,.

Due to
\frac{1}{p}-r=1+r^{\prime}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}
the assertion follows.
∎

3.1.2
Restriction to a subdomain

Let us next turn to Besov spaces of dominating mixed smoothness on subdomains
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, denoted by
S^{r}_{p,q}B(D)
. Those
are commonly defined in an extrinsic fashion, i.e. by restricting distributions
f\in S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
D
. In this paper we will a-priori only consider
the case

r>\sigma_{p}

with

\displaystyle\sigma_{p}:=\big(\tfrac{1}{p}-1\big)_{+}\,.

(24)

Then
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is comprised of regular distributions
f\in L_{1}^{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by Proposition
A.6
, which
simplifies the definition of
S^{r}_{p,q}B(D)
since the restriction operation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{D}:\;f\mapsto f|_{D}

(25)

is naturally defined on functions.
Specifically, we are interested in functions on the unit cube
[0,1]^{d}
.
Note that the restriction (
25
) of
f\in L^{loc}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
D=[0,1]^{d}
is realized by the operator
\mathcal{R}
from Definition
2.2
and leads to
a function in
L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
.
The scale we consider can hence be defined as follows.

Definition 3.6

(cf.
[
54
, App. B]
)
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r>\sigma_{p}
. The space
S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})
consists of all functions
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
for which an extension
F\in S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
exists with
f=F|_{[0,1]^{d}}
. The expression

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\in S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
F|_{[0,1]^{d}}=f\end{subarray}}\left\lVert F\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

is a (quasi-)norm in
S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})
.

Due to the extrinsic definition these spaces inherit many properties from the spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for example their completeness, which follows from standard arguments from functional analysis regarding quotient spaces.
Also the proposition below is a direct consequence of the extrinsic definition of
S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})
.

Proposition 3.7
.

For
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r>\sigma_{p}
, the restriction
\mathcal{R}:f\mapsto f|_{[0,1]^{d}}
from Definition
2.2
is a bounded operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:\;S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})\,.

For more information on Besov spaces on bounded domains
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
we refer to e.g.
[
57
, Sec. 1.2.6]
).

3.1.3
Periodic setting

At last, we recall the definition of the Besov spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
of dominating mixed smoothness on the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(see e.g.
[
60
, Sec. 4]
or
[
53
, Ch. 3]
).
As before, we only consider
the range
r>\sigma_{p}
, where they are, according to
[
60
, Lem. 3.2.2 & Rem. 4.2.1]
, embedded in
L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We hence only deal with
proper functions and not general distributions.
It is important to remark that there are two common ways to view the space
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
On the one hand, its elements are considered as functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. If we use the identification
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
those are functions on the domain
[-1,1]^{d}
. In an alternative viewpoint, those can also be understood as periodic functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, however, with the period
[-1,1]^{d}
.
Both viewpoints have their merits and we will notationally not distinguish between them.

Now we come to the definition of
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Since every function
f\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
corresponds to a periodic function in
L^{loc}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
the Littlewood-Paley blocks (
21
) are well-defined and the definition below makes sense.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r>\sigma_{p}
, and
\{\Phi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
a family of kernels associated to
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is defined as the space of all
f\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for which

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}2^{rq|\bar{j}|_{1}}\left\lVert\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f\right\rVert_{L_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\,,\quad&q<\infty\;,\\[17.22217pt]
\displaystyle\sup_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}2^{r|\bar{j}|_{1}}\left\lVert\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f\right\rVert_{L_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\;,\quad&q=\infty\,,\end{cases}

is finite. The expression
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
constitutes a (quasi-)norm in
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

As the spaces on the cube, these periodic spaces share many properties with
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For instance, their definition is independent of the chosen partition of unity
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in
the sense of equivalent (quasi-)norms. Also, they are
u
-Banach spaces for
u:=\min\{1,p,q\}
.

In the literature, see e.g.
[
17
, Def. 3.3.3]
or
[
60
, Def. 4.2.1]
, another form of the Littlewood-Paley blocks
\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f
is often used.
To derive this form, we first note that
every function
f\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
can be represented by a distributional Fourier series

f=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{f}(\bar{k})\exp_{\bar{k}}

with bounded coefficients
\{\hat{f}(\bar{k})\}_{\bar{k}}\in\ell_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
. The coefficients are thereby given by
\hat{f}(\bar{k})=\langle f,\overline{\exp}_{\bar{k}}\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}
with exponentials from the system
\mathcal{E}([-1,1]^{d})
defined in (
13
). Since the Fourier representation of
f
is weak* convergent in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can calculate

\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f(x)=\left\langle f,\Phi_{\bar{j}}(x-\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{f}(\bar{k})\left\langle\exp_{\bar{k}},\Phi_{\bar{j}}(x-\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\hat{f}(\bar{k})\,\varphi_{\bar{j}}\!\big(\bar{k}/2\big)\exp_{\bar{k}}(x)\,.

The last equality is due to (
20
) and

\left\langle\exp_{\bar{k}},\Phi_{\bar{j}}(x-\cdot)\right\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{S}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Phi_{\bar{j}}(x-\xi)\exp_{\bar{k}}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}2^{-d/2}\Phi_{\bar{j}}(\xi)\exp(\pi\mathrm{i}\bar{k}\cdot(x-\xi))\,\mathrm{d}\xi=\widehat{\Phi}_{\bar{j}}\!\big(\bar{k}/2\big)\exp_{\bar{k}}(x)\,.

Setting
\psi_{\bar{j}}:=\varphi_{\bar{j}}(\cdot/2)
we can thus write the Littlewood-Paley blocks (
21
) in the alternative form

\displaystyle f_{\bar{j}}=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\psi_{\bar{j}}(\bar{k})\hat{f}(\bar{k})\exp_{\bar{k}}\,,

(26)

where
\{\psi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}
still belongs to
\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since the definition of
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is independent of the utilized family in
\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can substitute
\psi_{\bar{j}}
by
\varphi_{\bar{j}}
in (
26
) and use this sum as an alternative to
\Phi_{\bar{j}}\ast f
in the definition of the (quasi-)norm of
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Let us finally have a look at the operator
\mathcal{R}:f\mapsto f|_{[0,1]^{d}}
from Definition
2.2
. Utilizing the identification

\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
, it can be applied to functions
f\in L_{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Recall Lemma
2.5
.
An important mapping property of
\mathcal{R}
for functions

f\in S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is given in Proposition
3.10
.
But let us first recall a result, which we will use in its proof.

Lemma 3.9

(
[
46
, Thm. 1.3]
)
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,

r>\sigma_{p}
. Let further
\psi\in C_{c}^{k}(K)
be a function with

k\geq\lfloor r\rfloor+1

for some compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then for all
f\in S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})

\displaystyle\|\psi\cdot f\|_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|f\|_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\,.

From this lemma we can deduce Proposition
3.10
, which is analogous
to Proposition
3.7
.
It concludes our introductory part on
classical Besov spaces.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r>\sigma_{p}
. With the identification
\mathbb{T}^{d}\cong[-1,1]^{d}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}:\;S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})\to S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})

is bounded.

Proof.

We interpret
f\in S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as a periodic function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and choose
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\psi(x)=1
on
[0,1]^{d}
. With Lemma
3.9
we obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|_{S^{r}_{p,q}B([0,1]^{d})}\leq\|\psi\cdot f\|_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|f\|_{S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\,.\qed

3.2
Half-period cosine spaces of dominating mixed Besov-type

The function spaces,
we are about to define in this subsection, are new and represent
a generalization of half-period cosine spaces considered earlier in the literature, e.g. in
[
16
,
21
]
.
They are of dominating mixed Besov-type and shall be denoted by
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
. Their
definition closely resembles that of the periodic spaces

S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

3.2.1
Definition

We restrict to spaces comprised of regular distributions, i.e. distributions that can be interpreted as proper functions. Since those are locally integrable, we can assume
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
.
Based on a decomposition of unity

\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we then
define the half-period cosine building blocks of
f
by

f^{\mathrm{hpc}}_{\bar{j}}:=\sum\limits_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\varphi_{\bar{j}}(\bar{k})\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k})c_{\bar{k}}\,,\quad\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\,,

(27)

with the half-period cosine coefficients
\hat{f}_{\rm hpc}(\bar{k}):=\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\rangle
as in (
10
).
The following lemma holds true.

Lemma 3.11
.

For any
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
we have the identity

f=\sum_{\bar{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}}f^{\mathrm{hpc}}_{\bar{j}}\quad\text{weak*ly in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,.

Proof.

Starting from Lemma
2.9
, we see that in the sense of tempered distributions we have

\displaystyle f=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}=\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\varphi_{\bar{j}}(\bar{k})\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}=\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\varphi_{\bar{j}}(\bar{k})\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle c_{\bar{k}}=\sum_{\bar{j}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{d}}f^{\mathrm{hpc}}_{\bar{j}}\,.

This calculation is justified by the fact that for every
\eta\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the series

\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\sum_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}\varphi_{\bar{j}}(\bar{k})\left\langle f,c_{\bar{k}}\right\rangle\left\langle c_{\bar{k}},\eta\right\rangle

is absolutely convergent.
∎

The definition of
f^{\mathrm{hpc}}_{\bar{j}}
in (
27
) is inspired by the form (
26
) of the Littlewood-Paley blocks
f_{\bar{j}}
and the expansion of
f
in Lemma
3.11
is analogous to the Littlewood-Paley expansion (
22
).
The half-period cosine spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}([0,1]^{d})

of dominating mixed Besov-type
are now defined in analogy to the periodic spaces
S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Definition 3.12

(Half-period cosine spaces of dominating mixed Besov-type)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{d}=\{c_{\bar{k}}\}_{\bar{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}}
be the half-period cosine system given in (
6
). Further, let
0<p,q\leq\infty
and
r>\sigma_{p}
. Then
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
is defined
as the collection of all
f\in L_{1}([0,1]^{d})
such that

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}2^{rq|\bar{j}|_{1}}\big\|f^{\mathrm{hpc}}_{\bar{j}}\big\|_{L_{p}([0,1]^{d})}^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\;,\quad&q<\infty\,,\\[17.22217pt]
\displaystyle\sup_{\bar{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}}2^{r|\bar{j}|_{1}}\big\|f^{\mathrm{hpc}}_{\bar{j}}\big\|_{L_{p}([0,1]^{d})}\;,\quad&q=\infty\,,\end{cases}

is finite. The expression
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)}
constitutes a (quasi-)norm in
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
.

It is not clear right from Definition
3.12
that these spaces are independent of the chosen system
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in (
27
). Also
other properties, such as the (quasi-)Banach space property, are not immediate.
However, these spaces fulfill analogous properties as their classical Besov space counterparts. This will be confirmed as a side result of Theorem
3.13
proved in the next subsection.

3.2.2
Identification

Our first main result on half-period cosine spaces, Theorem
3.13
, establishes an isomorphism that is essential for the periodization principle. It can be viewed as a refinement of Lemma
2.4
.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
0<p,q\leq\infty
,
r>\sigma_{p}
. Then

\mathcal{P}:\;S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)\cong S^{r}_{p,q}B(\mathbb{T}^{d})_{\rm even}\,.

Proof.

Let
\{\varphi_{\bar{j}}\}_{\bar{j}}\in\Phi_{\rm hyp}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a fixed decomposition of unity and
S^{r}_{p,q}B_{\mathrm{hpc}}\left([0,1]^{d}\right)
the associated half-period cosine space. The functions
\varphi_{\bar{j}}
have the structure

\varphi_{\bar{j}}=\varphi^{(1)}_{j_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\varphi^{(d)}_{j_{d}}
